**Summary.** This paper introduces Projected q-Shells (PQS), an advanced basis set technique designed to improve upon existing methods for electronic structure calculations. PQS enhances the accuracy and completeness of the basis set representation by mathematically restoring lost polynomial orders during the basis contraction process. This results in more accurate and efficient calculations for molecular electronic properties.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on quantum chemistry, the core mathematical machinery—developing highly accurate, compact, and systematically improvable basis sets for electron-electron interactions—is crucial for any simulation involving many-body quantum systems, including those studied in AMO physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Electronic-structure calculations require accurately representing both electronic wave functions and the Coulomb interaction using a finite basis set representation.

**Main result.** Projected q-Shells (PQS) offer an improvement over standard nested Gausslet constructions by approximately restoring the lost polynomial completeness order, leading to smaller computational errors for small molecules.

**Method.** The authors introduce PQS, an advanced basis set construction that improves upon nested Cartesian gausslets by using a projection technique to maintain high local polynomial completeness while keeping the interaction manageable.

**Model / system.** The method is applied to electronic structure calculations for atoms and molecules (e.g., H2+, H2, C2), focusing on approximating the computationally expensive four-index Coulomb interaction with a two-index form.

**Key observables.** One-electron and interaction errors; polynomial completeness order ($q$ vs $q-2$); mean Cartesian rms width ($\sigma^2$) and relative center shift ($d_{12}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The basis set parameter $q$ (related to polynomial order) and the degree of coordinate distortion.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The ideal model assumes undistorted parent lattices; practical use involves coordinate distortions, which slightly compromise the theoretical equivalence.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates four methods of spatial resolution change, including PQS compression. Figure 2 compares WL nesting (disjoint pieces) versus PQS (full contraction $ightarrow$ projection $ightarrow$ reorthogonalization) in 2D.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of basis set representation, details the limitations of standard nesting, mathematically derives the PQS construction via the projected-shell theorem, and validates the method by comparing PQS results against standard nesting for small molecules.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Electronic-structure calculations require a finite representation of both the electronic wave functions and their Coulomb interaction. Conventional compact basis sets lead to a four-index interaction. Gausslet basis sets are one of a very small number of approaches that allow a much smaller two-index interaction, similar to a real-space grid, but with far fewer points. The continuing challenge in their development is reducing further the number of functions needed while maintaining high accuracy. Here we introduce projected q-shells (PQS), an improvement to the nested Cartesian gausslets of White and Lindsey. Their standard nesting construction divides a joining shell into disjoint faces, edges, and corners, reducing its polynomial completeness from order q to order q-2. PQS approximately restores the two lost orders. In terms of completeness, we prove that for an ideal, undistorted parent, the span of the PQS basis is equivalent to an overcomplete basis of overlapping patches. Coordinate distortions, which are always used in practice, harm this equivalence only slightly. The moment properties that allow two-index interactions also become inexact, even without distortion, but we show that the improvement in order more than makes up for this loss. For similar basis sizes, PQS gives smaller one-electron and interaction errors on small molecules, such as H2+, H2, and C2, than standard nesting. The improvement is largest for more compact bases with smaller q.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops and proves rigorous geometric lower bounds for the Bures length in 1D chiral quantum systems at finite temperatures. By utilizing Uhlmann's framework, the authors establish bounds that smoothly connect the known zero-temperature topological constraints to the high-temperature limit. The results are validated using standard models like the SSH chain.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between quantum geometry (like Bures distance) and topological invariants at finite temperature is a fundamental, challenging problem in condensed matter theory, relevant to understanding thermalization and quantum information storage.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper establishes rigorous quantum geometric lower bounds for one-dimensional chiral-symmetric systems at finite temperature, addressing the ambiguity of such relations when temperature is non-zero.

**Main result.** Two temperature-dependent bounds are derived for the Bures length ($L_B$): one related to the Uhlmann phase angle ($\Theta$), and another involving the minimum energy gap ($\Delta_{\min}$), which interpolates between zero and high temperatures.

**Method.** The analysis uses Uhlmann's framework for mixed states to calculate the Bures metric and connection coefficients, deriving bounds through analytical manipulation of these geometric quantities.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on general one-dimensional (1D) chiral-symmetric systems, exemplified by the Su-Schrieffer-Heeger (SSH) model and the spinless Kitaev chain model.

**Key observables.** Bures length ($L_B$), Uhlmann phase angle ($\Theta$), and the temperature-dependent bound involving the minimum energy gap ($\Delta_{\min}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T$), minimum energy gap ($\Delta_{\min}$), and the ratio of coupling constants (e.g., $J_1/J_2$ for SSH).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to 1D chiral-symmetric systems, and some derivations rely on approximations like the QITE method for numerical verification.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison of the derived geometric bounds for $L_B$ versus system parameters and temperature, and show numerical verification of the Bures metric calculation using the QITE method.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the problem of finite-temperature geometric bounds, derives two distinct analytical bounds for the Bures length using Uhlmann's framework, and then verifies these results numerically using established models like the SSH chain.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The geometry and topology of quantum states are intimately related at zero temperature through exact bounds that constrain geometric quantities from below by topological invariants. At finite-temperature, however, the analogous relations remain unclear. Here we establish rigorous geometric lower bounds for one-dimensional (1D) chiral-symmetric systems at finite temperature within the Uhlmann's framework for mixed states. We show that the Bures length is bounded by a continuous geometric phase angle. We further derive a temperature-dependent bound that interpolates between the zero-temperature limit and a trivial high-temperature regime. Our results are verified analytically and numerically using the Su-Schrieffer-Heeger (SSH) model and the spinless Kitaev chain model. Finally, we discuss potential ways to detect the geometry of the density matrix in quantum circuits, with the quantum imaginary time evolution (QITE) method.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes an advanced framework for Quantum Federated Learning designed for noisy quantum hardware. It leverages the Bures metric and mean Uhlmann curvature—derived from analyzing mixed quantum states—to create a superior aggregation rule. This method dynamically weights clients based on their local noise and parameter incompatibility, leading to better model convergence than standard averaging techniques.

**Why it may be interesting.** It directly addresses the practical challenges of applying quantum machine learning algorithms to real, noisy hardware by incorporating advanced concepts from quantum information geometry into the optimization framework.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a robust aggregation rule for Quantum Federated Learning (QFL) that accurately accounts for data and hardware heterogeneity and noise in quantum devices.

**Main result.** The proposed method, utilizing the Bures metric and mean Uhlmann curvature, maintains high accuracy across diverse noise conditions and outperforms standard federated averaging, which degrades under strong noise.

**Method.** The authors extend quantum geometry from pure states to mixed states, employing the Bures metric as a local preconditioner and using the mean Uhlmann curvature to develop a dynamic, precision-aware aggregation rule.

**Model / system.** The model involves training variational quantum circuits (VQCs) on noisy quantum devices (NISQ era). Clients prepare mixed states $\rho_k(\theta)$ due to hardware noise, necessitating the use of mixed-state geometry.

**Key observables.** Bures metric (measures state change rate) and mean Uhlmann curvature (quantifies parameter estimation incompatibility).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Depolarizing rates (e.g., 0.4–0.6), data size ($p_k$), and local precision ($ho_k$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core novelty is moving the geometric analysis from pure states to the mixed-state regime, which is necessary because pure-state approaches discard crucial correlation information.

**Figures summary.** The notes detail comparisons of aggregation weights (QFedQGT vs QFedFisher vs QFedAvg) and the final accuracy results on a trapped-ion quantum emulator.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem, derives the mixed-state geometry (Bures-Uhlmann), proposes the local update (Bures metric) and aggregation rule (Uhlmann curvature), provides theoretical convergence guarantees, and validates the method via empirical simulation on a trapped-ion emulator.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum federated learning enables collaborative model training across quantum devices without sharing raw data, and it faces the data and hardware heterogeneity inherent to noisy quantum devices. Utilizing the quantum geometric tensor is a natural remedy, yet pure-state approaches and diagonal approximations discard the correlations that encode parameter incompatibility. To address this, we extend the parameter-space geometry to the mixed states that noisy clients actually prepare. The real part of the resulting mixed-state geometric tensor is the Bures metric, which measures how fast the physical state changes under parameter variation, and the imaginary part is the mean Uhlmann curvature, which quantifies the incompatibility of estimating multiple parameters simultaneously. Accordingly, we employ the Bures metric as a local preconditioner and use the mean Uhlmann curvature to develop an achievable-precision aggregation rule that dynamically down-weights unreliable clients. Furthermore, we establish theoretical guarantees by proving a convergence theorem and a variance-dominance proposition. Empirical evaluations on a trapped-ion quantum emulator demonstrate that the proposed method maintains high accuracy across diverse device-heterogeneity conditions and outperforms standard federated averaging, whose accuracy degrades under strong noise.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper addresses the pathological continuum limit of 3D U(1) lattice gauge theory by modifying the standard action to include a term dependent on lattice monopoles. By tuning two parameters ($eta$ and $\mu$) along a specific trajectory, the authors demonstrate that a physically meaningful constant ratio between glueball mass and string tension can be maintained. This suggests a viable regularization scheme for the theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on gauge theory, the concept of finding a 'line of constant physics' by tuning parameters to regularize a pathological limit is a general concept in critical phenomena and phase transitions relevant to statistical mechanics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The continuum limit of the three-dimensional compact U(1) lattice gauge theory is pathological because the ratio of glueball masses to the square root of the string tension vanishes.

**Main result.** By modifying the lattice action to couple to the total number of lattice monopoles, the authors found a line of constant physics where this ratio remains constant, suggesting a physically well-behaved continuum limit.

**Method.** The authors modified the Wilson action by adding a term proportional to the monopole number and tuned two parameters ($eta$ and $\mu$) simultaneously to trace this line of constant physics.

**Model / system.** The system is the three-dimensional compact U(1) lattice gauge theory. The model is the standard Wilson action extended by a term coupling to the total number of lattice monopoles, $S = -eta \sum 	ext{Re} P - \mu N_m$.

**Key observables.** Glueball masses ($m_0$), string tension ($\sqrt{\sigma}$), and the ratio $m_0/\sqrt{\sigma}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Lattice coupling $eta$, monopole coupling $\mu$, and the continuum limit ($eta 	o \infty$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the dilute monopole gas approximation, although the results suggest this approximation might be circumvented by the bounded nature of lattice monopole charges.

**Figures summary.** Not specified.

**Paper structure.** The paper first identifies the pathology of the standard continuum limit. It then introduces the modified action and demonstrates that tuning $eta$ and $\mu$ along a specific trajectory keeps the ratio $m_0/\sqrt{\sigma}$ constant, suggesting a regularization.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In three-dimensional compact U(1) lattice gauge theory color confinement can be understood analytically through the dynamics of magnetic monopoles. However, its continuum limit is pathological, since the ratio of the glueball masses to the square root of the string tension vanishes as the continuum limit is approached. We investigate a simple extension of the Wilson action in which the total number of lattice monopoles is coupled to an additional parameter $μ$. By tuning $β$ and $μ$ simultaneously, we identify a line of constant physics along which the ratio of the lightest glueball mass to the square root of the string tension remains constant. We further show that the same scaling is satisfied, within numerical uncertainties, by the other low-lying glueball masses considered in this work. Along this trajectory, the string tension in lattice units decreases with increasing $β$, thus suggesting that the modified lattice action may provide a regularization of three-dimensional compact U(1) gauge theory with a physically well-behaved continuum limit.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel, adaptive Bell-test protocol to certify high-dimensional entanglement in unknown quantum states. By extending CSPSA to use the CGLMP inequality, the authors bypass the need for full quantum state tomography. The method successfully demonstrates certified entanglement violations up to $d=8$ while showing superior resource scaling compared to traditional characterization techniques.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, resource-efficient alternative to full state tomography for verifying entanglement in complex, high-dimensional quantum systems, which is crucial for scalable quantum communication protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of characterizing high-dimensional entanglement in unknown quantum states, which is resource-intensive using standard quantum state tomography (QST).

**Main result.** They successfully developed and demonstrated an adaptive Bell-test protocol that certifies entanglement in high-dimensional qudits ($d=2$ to $d=8$) without needing to reconstruct the full density matrix.

**Method.** The method extends Complex Simultaneous Perturbation Stochastic Approximation (CSPSA) to utilize the Collins-Gisin-Linden-Massar-Popescu (CGLMP) inequality for bipartite qudits, allowing for direct, adaptive violation testing.

**Model / system.** The system involves bipartite qudits (high-dimensional quantum systems) whose entanglement is tested using the CGLMP inequality. The analysis considers both pure and mixed states, including isotropic mixed states.

**Key observables.** The primary observable is the CGLMP parameter ($S_d$), where a violation ($S_d > 2$) certifies non-local correlations. Other diagnostics include the one-sided lower confidence bound ($S_L^d$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimension of the qudits ($d$), the visibility of the mixed state, and the number of optimization iterations/runs are key parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that the measurement-setting cost for each CSPSA iteration is independent of the dimension $d$. The certification relies on a one-sided confidence bound derived from Hoeffding's inequality.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the resource costs (measurement configurations vs. detected pairs) between the proposed adaptive Bell search and Quantum State Tomography (QST) across different dimensions.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem, details the extension of CSPSA to high-dimensional Bell testing using the CGLMP inequality, presents the statistical framework for confidence-certified violation, and concludes with numerical demonstrations comparing its efficiency against QST.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Entangled photons play a crucial role in quantum applications, and determining and characterising their entanglement is vital to using them effectively. High-dimensional entangled states offer richer possibilities, but their additional measurement degrees of freedom make them increasingly demanding to characterise. However, adaptive Bell-test methods based on complex simultaneous perturbation stochastic approximation (CSPSA) have so far focused mainly on qubits. Here we numerically investigate a Bell-inequality-violation-based method for detecting entanglement in unknown quantum states. We extend CSPSA to high-dimensional Bell testing by using the Collins-Gisin-Linden-Massar-Popescu (CGLMP) inequality for bipartite qudits. The resulting protocol can detect Bell-nonlocal correlations in unknown entangled states, whether pure or mixed, without first reconstructing their density matrix. Using 100 optimisation iterations in each of 100 independent finite-shot runs per number of dimensions d, we demonstrate certified CGLMP violations throughout d=2-8. For isotropic mixed states tested at a visibility of just 0.05 above the standard-Fourier violation threshold, we likewise observe confidence-certified CGLMP violations throughout the range of d studied. We compare this direct stochastic approach with quantum state tomography, the standard method for characterising an unknown state. In the matched benchmark, CSPSA uses fewer measurement configurations per attempt from d=6, whereas tomography requires fewer detected pairs per certified result through d=8. We also derive the phase dependence of the CGLMP parameter and clarify the features of its landscape that govern the adaptive search. Because the measurement-setting cost of each CSPSA iteration is independent of dimension, the method offers a particularly attractive route to the certification of high-dimensional entanglement.

</details>

**Summary.** This work models organizational efficiency by treating task allocation as an evolutionary process on a rugged fitness landscape, inspired by the NK model. It analyzes the trade-off between specialized and generalist labor forces to determine optimal strategies for maximizing productivity. The findings offer mathematical insights into how system complexity and interdependencies drive adaptive organizational change.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on socioeconomics, the underlying mathematical machinery—using fitness landscapes, group selection, and adaptive dynamics—is directly analogous to models used in non-equilibrium statistical mechanics and complex systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how the organizational division of labor and task interdependencies affect overall productivity and the capacity for adaptation in complex systems.

**Main result.** The model reveals the emergence of nonlinear adaptive dynamics, providing insights into how companies can adapt their strategies and foster innovation by optimizing labor division.

**Method.** The study employs group selection algorithms applied to a modified NK model, which simulates fitness landscapes shaped by task interdependencies.

**Model / system.** The model uses an NK-inspired framework applied to organizational structure, distinguishing between 'specialists' (fixed tasks) and 'generalists' (stochastic task selection). Productivity is optimized by balancing these two workforce types against task interdependencies.

**Key observables.** Organizational productivity, deviation from optimal labor division, and the emergence of nonlinear adaptive dynamics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Ruggedness of the productivity landscape (related to task interdependency), generalization threshold ($	heta$), and the exploration rate ($\mu$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often reduces to a minimal two-role setting for tractability, and empirical data suggests department size alone is not the primary predictor of performance.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates employee attribute profiles and interdependencies in a company setting. Figure 2 shows department workforce fraction vs. normalized performance across empirical datasets.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from an initial conceptual model (NK landscape applied to labor) to a detailed mathematical framework involving task allocation (specialist/generalist classification) and group selection evolution, with extensions proposed for dynamic market demands.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Specialization and task allocation enhance efficiency and innovation across diverse systems, from biological organisms to socioeconomic institutions. The evolution of task distribution and its influence on organizational productivity encapsulate the dynamics between task dependencies and adaptive strategies. We explore the organizational division of labor, inspired by the NK model of rugged landscapes, which is widely applied in evolutionary biology, and incorporate interdependencies among the attributes of technical experts within an organization. Our model considers two types of employees characterized by their task allocation strategies: specialists, who are permanently assigned to a single task, and generalists, who stochastically select a task at each time step. We investigate how the ruggedness of the productivity landscape, shaped by task interdependency, affects the organization's capacity to optimize labor division and meet market demands. Using group selection algorithms, we reveal the emergence of nonlinear adaptive dynamics, providing insights into how companies can adapt their strategies to meet market demands and foster innovation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper uses Path-integral Monte Carlo simulations to study how confining electrons in thin slabs affects their optical response. It demonstrates that the boundaries break the ideal Drude behavior, leading to measurable scattering effects quantified by fitting the response to Drude and Drude-Lorentz models. The findings are important for understanding charge transport in nanoscale quantum materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, first-principles simulation of how geometric confinement and quantum statistics modify fundamental optical properties, which is crucial for designing nanoscale electronic devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates quantum many-body effects on the long-wavelength optical response of confined electrons at finite temperatures.

**Main result.** Slab confinement significantly breaks down the ideal Drude response, causing measurable scattering effects, particularly in the out-of-plane optical susceptibility.

**Method.** The authors employ the Path-integral Monte Carlo (PIMC) method to simulate the system's dynamics and analyze the optical response via the imaginary-time dipole autocorrelation function.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is an ideal homogeneous electron gas (HEG) confined within a quasi-2D slab geometry, allowing for the study of boundary effects on electron interactions.

**Key observables.** Optical susceptibility $\chi(\omega)$, the imaginary-time dipole autocorrelation function $	ilde{G}(	au)$, and fitted parameters for the Drude ($\Gamma_D$) and Drude-Lorentz ($\Gamma_{DL}, \omega_0$) models.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Slab thickness ($2R$), particle density ($r_s$), finite temperature ($\Theta$), and the ratio of confinement size to thermal wavelength ($2R/\Lambda$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on fitting the PIMC data to Drude/Drude-Lorentz models for analytic continuation, and the PIMC method itself is limited by the Fermion sign problem.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dependence of fitted parameters ($\Gamma_D, \Gamma_{DL}, \omega_0$) on slab thickness relative to thermal wavelength and density, and compare results between fermionic and classical (Boltzmannon) simulations.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem by noting the breakdown of the ideal Drude response due to confinement. It details the PIMC methodology for calculating the optical response, presents quantitative results by fitting the imaginary-time correlation function to established models, and concludes by comparing the effects of quantum statistics and geometry.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study quantum many-body effects in the long-wavelength optical response of confined electrons at finite temperatures. We simulate homogeneous electron gas confined in one dimension into a slab of nanoscale thickness. We demonstrate how the slab boundaries break down the ideal Drude response of free charge carriers, giving rise to scattering effects due to both the surfaces and quantum many-body interactions. We use a recent path-integral Monte Carlo (PIMC) approach developed in [Tiihonen et al. Phys. Rev. A 113, 053711] to quantify these effects in high accuracy. We perform phenomenological fits to Drude and Drude-Lorentz models parameters, manifesting various trends of the optical response with physical parameters like density and temperature, and numerical effects like finite size and the quantum statistics.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates the thermodynamic efficiency of a simulated quantum-like computer performing ML classification. By analyzing the system's dynamics under stochastic thermodynamics, the authors show that the computer can operate close to theoretical efficiency limits. The findings reveal that the operational protocols offer significant flexibility in balancing speed, accuracy, and energy dissipation.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, non-quantum example of applying fundamental non-equilibrium thermodynamic bounds (like speed limits) to assess the computational limits of physical hardware, which has parallels in open quantum system dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper assesses the thermodynamic efficiency of a simulated thermodynamic computer performing a machine-learning classification task.

**Main result.** Different inference protocols allow the computer to operate near the thermodynamic efficiency limit without losing accuracy, indicating considerable operational freedom.

**Method.** The study uses stochastic thermodynamics, specifically analyzing the Wasserstein speed limit and comparing it to the actual entropy production rate.

**Model / system.** The system is a stochastic physical device modeled by an overdamped Langevin equation, trained to classify MNIST images using a potential energy function with multiple interaction terms.

**Key observables.** Classification accuracy, $L_2$-Wasserstein distance ($W_2$), and thermodynamic efficiency ($\eta$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Thermal energy scale ($k_B T$), runtime ($	au$), and the loading rate ($\Gamma_\ell(t)$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the Langevin equation approximation and uses Gaussian bounds for entropy production for practical calculations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the time evolution of classification accuracy, showing a peak at $	au=1/5$, and compare efficiency bounds under different loading protocols and time rescalings ($\lambda$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the basic performance metrics, then analyzing the effect of the loading protocol on efficiency, and finally examining the implications of the 'clock-acceleration procedure' on thermodynamic constraints.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Thermodynamic computers are stochastic physical devices designed to perform calculations at the thermal energy scale. Their operation is constrained by the equations of stochastic thermodynamics, among which are a set of bounds, known as speed limits, that relate a thermodynamic computer's run time to its computational progress and the heat it dissipates. Using the Wasserstein speed limit we assess the thermodynamic efficiency of a simulation model of a thermodynamic computer trained to perform a standard machine-learning classification task. On this task the thermodynamic computer is as capable as a simple multilayer perceptron. We show that different inference protocols allow the computer to operate within 40\% of the thermodynamic limit of efficiency without loss of accuracy, or to perform inference increasingly rapidly at fixed accuracy and thermodynamic efficiency. These results indicate that a thermodynamic computer designed for a particular task retains considerable freedom in its thermodynamic operation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a quantum generative model designed to create realistic synthetic patient data for rare diseases where real data is scarce. It leverages quantum circuits, trained on maximum entropy principles, to model complex probability distributions. Results show this quantum approach outperforms established classical generative models in fidelity and expressivity metrics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the application is biomedical, the core methodology involves advanced quantum circuit design, state preparation (MPS), and benchmarking against classical ML models, which is highly relevant to quantum algorithm development for complex data distributions.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is generating high-fidelity synthetic patient data for *in silico* clinical trials, especially when training datasets are scarce due to rare or heterogeneous diseases.

**Main result.** The proposed quantum generative model surpasses classical generative models in both generalization and expressivity metrics, indicating its potential for generating high-fidelity patient data.

**Method.** The authors propose a hybrid quantum-classical pipeline that maps the target joint probability distribution into a quantum circuit for sampling.

**Model / system.** The model uses a quantum generative approach, encoding the joint probability distribution into a quantum state, compressing it via a Matrix Product State (MPS), and executing the resulting circuit on a superconducting quantum computer (IBM Heron r2).

**Key observables.** Generalization metrics include Mean Absolute Error (MAE) and Jensen–Shannon Divergence (JSD); expressivity is measured by Random Forest classification accuracy, AUC, and Coverage Ratio.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The study tests performance across varying subsample sizes ($n_{	ext{selected}} \in \{100, 150, 200, 300, 400\}$) of the scarce data.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary limitations are the current noise levels and limited qubit counts of quantum devices, necessitating error-detection and post-selection steps.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare MAE/JSD and Accuracy/AUC vs. sample size, showing the quantum model's consistent superiority over classical baselines. Figure 4 displays confusion matrices confirming the quantum model's difficulty in distinguishing real from synthetic samples.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of data scarcity in clinical trials, details the quantum generative pipeline (encoding via MPS, circuit mapping), compares performance against classical GANs/VAEs using multiple metrics, and concludes with empirical results demonstrating quantum superiority.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In silico methods have emerged as a strategy to complement clinical trials. These are particularly relevant for rare or heterogeneous diseases for which traditional methods are costly or difficult to apply. While classical generative models have shown an extremely good ability to generate high fidelity data when trained using extensive databases, they often struggle when the available samples for training are scarce. In this work, we leverage the potential of quantum computers to represent complex probability distributions to generate high fidelity in silico patients. We propose a pipeline able to combine asymmetric databases into a quantum circuit that serves as a quantum generative model. We evaluate the efficacy of our proposal using a database of Myelodysplastic Syndrome (MDS) patients with 7 clinical variables as a proof-of-concept. We executed our quantum generative model in the IBM Heron r2 ``ibm\_basquecountry'' superconducting quantum computer and compare our method with well known classical baselines. Our results show that the quantum generative model surpasses the classical generative models in generalization and expressivity metrics, indicating its potential validity to generate high fidelity in silico patients for clinical trials.

</details>

**Summary.** This work proposes using quantum computation to calculate scattering cross sections for processes like Drell-Yan scattering by encoding Feynman diagrams into quantum gates. The authors develop a general circuit that can simultaneously compute the total cross-section and isolate interference effects by leveraging quantum superposition over phase space coordinates.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on particle physics, the core contribution is the development of a general, scalable quantum circuit framework for simulating complex quantum field theory amplitudes, which is a major computational primitive for quantum algorithms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of efficiently calculating complex scattering cross sections, particularly including interference effects, by mapping Feynman diagram calculations onto quantum circuits.

**Main result.** They demonstrate a general quantum circuit design capable of simultaneously estimating the full integrated cross-section and isolating interference terms for processes like Drell-Yan scattering.

**Method.** The methodology involves constructing specialized quantum gates (Vertex, Propagator, Spinor) that encode Feynman rules, and then using quantum superposition and basis rotations to evaluate phase space integrals and interference terms.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the partonic Drell-Yan lepton pair creation process ($qar{q} ightarrow \gamma/Z ightarrow \ell^-\ell^+$) within the Standard Model electroweak theory. The computation is performed using quantum circuits simulating the underlying quantum field theory interactions.

**Key observables.** Full integrated cross-section ($\sigma$) and the isolated interference contribution ($	ext{Int} \langle M_i, M_j angle$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The process depends on the interplay between photon ($\gamma$) and $Z$ boson contributions, and the calculation requires discretizing the phase space over energy ($\sqrt{s}$) and scattering angle ($	heta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial focus is restricted to the partonic level, neglecting Parton Distribution Functions (PDFs). Furthermore, unitarity requires defining a 'pole factor roof' $ar{m}$ for rescaling.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the decomposition of fundamental gates (Dirac matrices, $\eta$ gate, spinor gates, Vertex, Propagator) and show the full quantum circuit for the DY process, including PS control registers $E$ and $\Theta$.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically details the construction of necessary quantum gates (Dirac matrices, metric, spinor states, vertices, propagators) into fundamental quantum gates, followed by demonstrating the full circuit for the DY process, and finally showing how PS integration and interference isolation are achieved.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We probe the possibilities of efficiently constructing simple Feynman diagrams into quantum devices. More precisely, we study Drell-Yan lepton pair creation at the partonic level of the form q qbar -> gamma/Z -> l- l+. We develop quantum gates that build up the relevant diagrams using simple Feynman rules, such as vertex and propagator gates V and P. We show how the quantum circuit may compute simultaneous amplitudes in the phase space and how to reach the full integrated cross section from the outputs. In addition to this, we also show how the circuit is able to simultaneously isolate the interference effects of the contributing diagrams by a simple basis rotation. The circuit design is made to be general, and thus this work constitutes a step towards the implementation of arbitrary scattering process computations and efficient interference analyses.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the computational bottleneck in Tensor Network Variational Monte Carlo (TN-VMC) by developing a vectorized framework optimized for GPUs. By reformulating tensor network operations into a 'flat' formalism, the authors enable batch processing of multiple Monte Carlo configurations. This results in dramatic speedups for simulating fermionic systems like the Fermi-Hubbard model, advancing the feasibility of studying strongly correlated materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The focus on GPU acceleration and vectorization techniques for simulating strongly correlated electron models is highly relevant for computational quantum physics, providing concrete algorithmic improvements for simulating Hubbard-type Hamiltonians.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is improving the computational efficiency of Tensor Network Variational Monte Carlo (TN-VMC) calculations by enabling full utilization of modern GPU acceleration.

**Main result.** The authors developed a vectorized, GPU-adapted workflow using a 'flat' symmetric tensor formalism, achieving significant speedups (up to $300	imes$) over CPU implementations for fermionic systems.

**Method.** The core method involves vectorizing the evaluation of tensor network amplitudes by batching computations over multiple Markov chains (configurations) that share the same underlying tensor structure.

**Model / system.** The work is applied to strongly correlated spin and fermionic systems, with the two-dimensional Fermi–Hubbard model serving as the primary demonstration case. The variational wavefunctions are represented by Tensor Networks (TNS).

**Key observables.** Variational energy $E(	heta)$, wavefunction amplitudes $\Psi_	heta(\sigma)$, and computational speedup factors.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The batch size $B$ (number of configurations processed simultaneously) and the structure of the tensor network (e.g., abelian symmetric vs. general).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that the residual contraction graph for a set of configurations can be grouped by geometry, allowing for a shared computational graph suitable for dense batched tensor operations.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the transition from traditional MPI-parallelization to an efficient, vectorized GPU scheme by batching amplitude evaluations. Other figures detail the structure of tensor block representations and contraction schemes.

**Paper structure.** The paper first identifies the GPU bottleneck in existing TN-VMC. It then introduces the 'flat' symmetric tensor formalism to enable vectorization. The methodology is detailed by showing how to batch amplitude evaluations, which is demonstrated on the Fermi-Hubbard model, leading to performance benchmarks and extensions to other TN-VMC applications.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Variational Monte Carlo (VMC) calculations based on tensor networks (TN) have recently achieved competitive accuracy in ground-state calculations of strongly correlated spin and fermionic systems. However, existing tensor network VMC (TN-VMC) algorithms have not been formulated in a manner that can fully utilize GPU acceleration. We tackle the key missing ingredient, namely, the vectorized evaluation of tensor network amplitudes and tensor network operations. This ensures high GPU utilization by batching over computations with identical structure. In particular, we show how to achieve vectorization for the practically relevant case of abelian symmetric tensor networks (which includes fermionic tensor networks) by developing a ``flat'' tensor network formalism for block-sparse tensor representation and contraction. Using this, we construct a GPU-adapted symmetric TN-VMC workflow with batched tensor network computation. In the two-dimensional Fermi--Hubbard model, we demonstrate a GPU speedup of up to $300 \times$ over single core CPU implementations, for fermionic TN variational wavefunctions.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper demonstrates a high-performance computing solution for decoding quantum error-correcting codes using a DPU-based Processing-in-Memory (PIM) architecture. By mapping the iterative Belief Propagation decoding onto this near-memory platform, the authors achieve significant throughput gains and maintain predictable, low compute latency required for real-time quantum operations. This validates PIM as a viable accelerator for next-generation quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it tackles the computational bottleneck of fault-tolerant quantum computation by proposing a hardware acceleration strategy (PIM) for decoding algorithms central to QEC.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the need for classical decoders to process growing syndrome workloads in real-time quantum error correction (QEC) with low and predictable latency.

**Main result.** Implementing normalized Min-Sum Belief Propagation decoding on a DPU-based Processing-in-Memory (PIM) architecture yields an 8.8x throughput improvement over CPU baselines and maintains sub-millisecond compute latency.

**Method.** The authors mapped the iterative Min-Sum BP decoding algorithm onto a DPU-based PIM architecture, leveraging massive parallelism by distributing independent syndrome instances across thousands of DPUs.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on decoding for Quantum Low-Density Parity-Check (qLDPC) codes, specifically the [[144,12,12]] Bivariate Bicycle code. The computation is modeled using a DPU-based PIM architecture, which allows for near-memory processing.

**Key observables.** Throughput (decodes/s), per-syndrome processing time, single-syndrome tail latency, and Logical Error Rate (LER).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Component-wise physical error probability (p=0.001), number of BP iterations (1 to 5), and the 1 ms decoder-side timing reference for trapped-ion QEC.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The DPU latency measured is the decoder-side compute time, excluding host-DPU communication overhead. The Min-Sum BP normalization factor is fixed at 0.75 to enable integer arithmetic.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the qLDPC code structure (Tanner graph) and compare the decoding workflows and latency distributions between the CPU and the proposed DPU-based PIM architecture.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the QEC decoding bottleneck, details the mapping of Min-Sum BP to the DPU-PIM architecture, presents quantitative comparisons of throughput and latency against a CPU baseline, and concludes with performance validation across varying error rates and iterations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Real-time quantum error correction requires classical decoders to process growing syndrome workloads with low and predictable latency. For quantum low-density parity-check (qLDPC) codes, iterative belief propagation (BP) repeatedly updates messages over sparse Tanner graphs, creating substantial memory-access and data-movement demands. We map normalized Min-Sum BP decoding of the [[144,12,12]] Bivariate Bicycle qLDPC code onto a DPU-based Processing-in-Memory (PIM) architecture. Within each DPU, 11 tasklets cooperatively decode one syndrome, while multiple DPUs process independent syndrome instances in parallel. Using uPIMulator and a data-qubit Pauli error model with ideal syndrome measurements, we compare throughput, per-syndrome processing time, logical error rate (LER), and single-syndrome tail latency against a 16-logical-CPU baseline. At a component-wise physical error probability of p=0.001 and one BP iteration, the projected aggregate kernel throughput of 2,560 DPUs reaches 1.071 x 10^7 decodes/s, compared with 1.22 x 10^6 decodes/s for the CPU, an 8.8x improvement. From two iterations onward, the measured LER remains below the physical error probability for every evaluated value of p. For one to five iterations, the maximum sampled serialized X+Z DPU compute latency remains below the 1 ms decoder-side reference for trapped-ion QEC, reaching approximately 0.873 ms at five iterations. These results show that near-memory processing can provide high aggregate throughput and sub-millisecond compute latency for qLDPC BP decoding under the evaluated conditions.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes a hybrid quantum amplifier that uses a finite-cutoff quantum-scissor mechanism to achieve noiseless amplification. While the ideal theory predicts perfect enhancement, the finite cutoff introduces phase dependence and limits fidelity. The results show that while the hybrid device offers a heralding advantage, maintaining high fidelity requires careful management of the input state phase and increasing the cutoff.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the practical limitations of achieving ideal quantum operations (like noiseless amplification) by modeling the effects of physical constraints, such as finite energy cutoffs, which is a key topic in quantum optics and quantum information.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the performance of a hybrid noiseless linear amplifier (NLA) that incorporates a finite-cutoff quantum-scissor NLA placed between two single-mode squeezers.

**Main result.** The hybrid device shows a significant heralding probability advantage over a bare NLA, but its fidelity is highly sensitive to the input phase and degrades as the input squeezing increases due to the finite cutoff.

**Method.** The analysis uses Fock-basis expansions to model the state evolution through the hybrid amplifier, comparing the resulting effective gain, success probability, and fidelity against the ideal, infinite-cutoff case.

**Model / system.** The system is a quantum optical setup comprising two single-mode squeezers sandwiching a finite-cutoff NLA, which truncates the photon Fock space up to a maximum number $N$.

**Key observables.** Effective gain ($G$), Success probability ($P$), and Fidelity ($F$) are the primary observables used to characterize the amplification process.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The cutoff photon number ($N$), the squeezing parameters ($r, r'$), and the input coherent amplitude phase ($\alpha$) are crucial parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The ideal NLA is assumed to have zero success probability over the full Fock space, and the finite cutoff introduces phase dependence and fidelity loss.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the effective gain, success probability, and fidelity of the hybrid device versus the bare NLA at various cutoffs (e.g., cutoff 11) and input squeezing parameters ($\chi$).

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the ideal infinite-cutoff transformation, then analyzes the finite-cutoff device, demonstrating how gain enhancement emerges with increasing cutoff, and finally comparing the performance metrics (P and F) against the bare NLA.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A noiseless linear amplifier (NLA) can probabilistically amplify an optical state without the noise required by deterministic phase-insensitive amplification. We study a hybrid amplifier in which a finite-cutoff quantum-scissor NLA is placed between two single-mode squeezers. Analysis based on an ideal (infinite cutoff) NLA finds a gain enhancement from the squeezing. We explore the physics of this enhancement as the cutoff of the quantum scissors is increased. The low-cutoff sequence shows how this gain enhancement emerges from the truncated Fock space. Cutoff 3 is the lowest order at which the additional even- and odd-photon components can both contribute to gain enhancement, albeit with some skewing of the coefficients which reduces the fidelity. As the cutoff is increased the fidelity improves. However, unlike the ideal transformation, the finite-cutoff device depends on the phase of the coherent amplitude relative to the squeezing axes, with states aligned with the anti-squeezing requiring higher cutoffs to achieve high fidelity. We track behavior to high cutoffs and eventually see the performance predicted in the ideal theory emerge.

</details>

**Authors:** Daesik Kim, Sumin Choi, Hyojae Jeon, Jung Hoon Han  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** disordered systems and neural networks · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.28059v1>

When we take the γ →0 limit first, the crossover line becomes the exact phase transition. Furthermore, the nonanalytic critical point (NCP) at (κ,τ) = (1,1) develops new critical exponents (βcr,δcr,γcr) = (2,3/2,1) different from the rest of the phase diagram where (βcr,δcr,γcr) = (1,2,1).</sub>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the interpolation singularity in linear in-context learning by mapping the problem onto the framework of critical phenomena in disordered systems. Using a Landau theory approach, the authors derive critical exponents and predict the behavior of the system near the singularity. This work provides a solid statistical-physics understanding of how learning capacity limits manifest in modern AI models.

**Why it may be interesting.** The application of established critical phenomena tools (Landau theory, critical exponents) to the dynamics of deep learning models provides a powerful, physics-grounded framework for understanding generalization and learning capacity.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the interpolation singularity in linear in-context learning (ICL) by treating it as a critical phenomenon in a quenched disordered system.

**Main result.** A Landau theory is constructed, predicting critical exponents $(eta_{cr}, \delta_{cr}, \gamma_{cr}) = (1, 2, 1)$ for the singularity, which is confirmed by numerical simulations.

**Method.** The authors compare annealed and quenched descriptions of the linear ICL model, constructing a Landau potential from the cavity self-consistency equation.

**Model / system.** The model is linear ICL, where prediction error exhibits a double-descent singularity when pretraining samples approach the number of parameters. The analysis uses concepts from statistical physics, mapping ICL parameters to temperature and magnetic field variables.

**Key observables.** Landau susceptibility, order parameter ($\xi$), and the prediction error ($E$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Normalized sample complexity ($	au$), context length ($\alpha$), task diversity ($\kappa$), and the critical temperature ($	au_c = 1$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory assumes that connected sample-to-sample fluctuations are the microscopic origin of the singular error, and it relies on asymptotic limits ($d 	o \infty$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the order parameter $\xi^*$ in the $(\kappa, 	au)$ plane, showing the true critical line ($	au=1$) and crossover line ($	au=\kappa$), and map the numerically extracted power-law exponents $p$ in the scaling relation $\xi^* \propto \gamma^p$.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a structure detailing the formulation, annealed theory, quenched cavity solution, Landau functional construction, numerical testing, and final summary.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In-context learning (ICL) allows a pretrained model to infer a new task from examples supplied in its prompt without updating its parameters. In linear models of ICL, the prediction error develops a double-descent singularity when the number of pretraining samples becomes comparable to the number of learnable parameters. We formulate this interpolation singularity as a critical phenomenon of a quenched disordered system. By comparing annealed and quenched descriptions of the same linear ICL model, we identify the connected sample-to-sample fluctuations of the learned parameters as the microscopic origin of the singular error. A Landau potential is constructed by integrating the cavity self-consistency equation for the renormalized ridge parameter $ξ$. The role of (magnetization) order parameter is played by $ξ$, while the bare ridge parameter $λ$ becomes its conjugate magnetic field. The normalized sample complexity $τ$ acts as a temperature and the double-descent singularity occurs at the critical temperature $τ_c =1$. The Landau susceptibility is precisely the quantity that diverges in the fluctuation contribution to the prediction error. The order parameter is closely related to the fraction of zero eigenvalues of the empirical relaxation matrix in the ridgeless limit, which define flat directions in the learning dynamics. The Landau theory is generically cubic in the order parameter with critical exponents $(β_{\rm cr},δ_{\rm cr},γ_{\rm cr})=(1,2,1)$. In the large-context regime, there appears a pseudogap-like regime characterized by suppressed order parameter. Predictions of the Landau theory are independently confirmed from numerical solutions of the original learning problem with good quantitative agreement. Our results pave the way for solid statistical-physics understanding of the interpolation criticality in linear in-context learning.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Logical Neural Belief Propagation (L-NBP), a novel decoder for surface codes that improves quantum error correction performance. L-NBP achieves this by training a neural network to decode directly at the logical level, rather than the physical error level. It successfully maintains the linear complexity of Belief Propagation while matching or exceeding the accuracy of more complex, non-scalable decoders.

**Why it may be interesting.** The combination of classical graph algorithms (BP) with modern machine learning techniques (Neural Networks) to solve a fundamental problem in quantum computation is highly relevant for developing practical quantum hardware.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing quantum error correction (QEC) decoders that achieve high logical accuracy while maintaining linear complexity scaling with code length.

**Main result.** The proposed L-NBP decoder matches or outperforms state-of-the-art decoders (MWPM, BP-OSD) while retaining the linear complexity of Belief Propagation.

**Method.** It uses a Neural Belief Propagation (NBP) module to generate soft posterior beliefs, which are then fed into a trainable logical classifier to predict the logical operator end-to-end.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on decoding surface codes, which are leading candidates for QEC due to their local connectivity and high error threshold.

**Key observables.** Logical Error Rate (LER), computational complexity (FLOPs), and the soft syndrome $\tilde{s}$ used for classification.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Depolarizing noise threshold (achieved 17.5%), code distance ($d=9$), and the linear complexity scaling ($O(n)$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that training the decoder end-to-end on the logical objective is necessary for optimal performance.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the QEC workflow, the comparison of FLOPs scaling between L-NBP and BP-OSD across different noise models, and t-SNE plots showing the separation of logical operators in the soft syndrome space.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem, details the L-NBP architecture (NBP $\rightarrow$ Soft Syndrome $\rightarrow$ Logical Classifier), compares its performance and complexity against established decoders (MWPM, BP-OSD), and validates the end-to-end training objective.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum error correction (QEC) requires decoders that achieve high logical accuracy while scaling efficiently with the code length. Belief propagation (BP) is attractive for its linear decoding complexity, but conventional BP decoders often fail to reach sufficient logical accuracy on surface codes. We propose Logical Neural Belief Propagation (L-NBP), a BP-based neural decoder that redirects the decoding objective from physical-level decoding to logical-level decoding. L-NBP first runs a neural BP (NBP) module that produces posterior beliefs, and a logical classifier then transforms these beliefs into a continuous-valued soft syndrome and predicts the logical operator. Because all components in L-NBP are trainable by backpropagation, L-NBP is trained end-to-end, so that the NBP module learns to extract soft syndromes that are favorable for logical classification. On surface codes, L-NBP matches or outperforms the BP with ordered-statistics decoding (BP-OSD) and minimum-weight perfect matching (MWPM) while retaining the linear complexity of BP, and achieves a threshold of $17.5\%$ under depolarizing noise. Moreover, under circuit-level noise, L-NBP matches the accuracy of BP-OSD on the distance-$9$ surface code while requiring only $0.2\%$ of its complexity. These results show that combining BP, neural weights, and logical-level decoding enables scalable and high-accuracy quantum decoding.

</details>

### [Quantum Resource Estimation for Simulating the SYK Model with Trotterization, qDRIFT, and Asymmetric Qubitization](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27573v1)

**Authors:** Brian Goldsmith, Larissa Kroell, Nishna Aerabati  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.27573v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `analog quantum simulation` **1/5**

**Summary.** This work compares three methods—Trotterization, qDRIFT, and asymmetric qubitization—for simulating the complex SYK model on quantum hardware. By estimating qubit and T gate counts, the authors demonstrate that while some methods are qubit-efficient, asymmetric qubitization offers superior resource scaling, particularly concerning the number of required T gates. This provides a crucial resource guide for future quantum simulation efforts.

**Why it may be interesting.** The comparison of resource scaling across different quantum simulation techniques (Trotter vs. QSP-based) provides concrete, actionable data for researchers designing quantum algorithms for strongly correlated quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the critical need to understand the resource requirements for simulating the Sachdev-Ye-Kitaev (SYK) model on early fault-tolerant quantum computers.

**Main result.** While qDRIFT and Trotterization use fewer qubits, asymmetric qubitization proves advantageous in most cases due to the significantly lower required count of T gates.

**Method.** The authors compare three distinct quantum simulation techniques—Trotterization, qDRIFT, and asymmetric qubitization utilizing Quantum Signal Processing (QSP)—to estimate quantum resources.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on simulating the SYK model, a system of interacting Majorana fermions, which is theoretically important due to its connection to holographic duality and AdS/CFT correspondence.

**Key observables.** Qubit count and T gate count, analyzed as functions of simulation precision ($\epsilon_{alg}$) and the number of Majorana modes ($N$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Precision ($\epsilon_{alg}$), number of Majorana modes ($N$), and simulation time ($t$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The resource estimates are based on theoretical scaling analyses of the three distinct Hamiltonian simulation methods.

**Figures summary.** Figure 2 compares T gate scaling ($\epsilon$ dependence) showing QSP's superior scaling, while Figure 3 compares qubit count and T gate scaling ($N$ dependence) showing AQ's advantage in T gate count.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the SYK model and the simulation challenge, details the three methods (Trotterization, qDRIFT, AQ/QSP), presents resource estimates for qubit and T gates, and concludes with a comparison of the scaling laws.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Sachdev-Ye-Kitaev (SYK) model has been identified as a promising candidate to run on early fault-tolerant quantum computers due to the relatively modest resources required to probe non-trivial physics (namely holographic duality and AdS/CFT correspondence). As such, it is crucial that the details of how to run such a simulation are well understood. Using PsiQuantum's Construct platform, we implement and analyze three different approaches to simulate the SYK model: Trotterization, qDRIFT, and asymmetric qubitization with Quantum Signal Processing. We provide an open-source library containing implementations for SYK simulation using all three methods, which we use to obtain quantum resource estimates for qubit and T gate count as functions of the number of Majorana modes and precision. We find that while qDRIFT and Trotterization benefit from a lower qubit count, the large number of T gates required lead to asymmetric qubitization being advantageous in most cases. This reinforces previous theoretical considerations. We intend both the implementations and the estimates to be useful for researchers to continue to study the SYK model and understand how the techniques and resources vary.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops recovery theorems for generalized Rényi divergences in the context of von Neumann algebras. It characterizes when the data processing inequality becomes an equality, linking this condition directly to the concept of a 'sufficient' quantum map. This provides deep mathematical insight into information preservation under general quantum operations.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides rigorous mathematical tools to quantify how much information about a quantum state is preserved (or recoverable) when the state undergoes a general physical process (the map $T$), which is fundamental to quantum channel characterization.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to establish recovery theorems for $\alpha$-$z$ Rényi divergences when applied to quantum states undergoing evolution via normal unital positive maps between von Neumann algebras.

**Main result.** Equality in the data processing inequality for these divergences is shown to be equivalent to the map being 'sufficient' with respect to the pair of states, which characterizes the recovery of information.

**Method.** The proof relies on analyzing the equality case of the data processing inequality using $L^p$-spaces associated with $JW^*$-subalgebras and generalizing techniques from the 2-positive setting to mere positivity.

**Model / system.** The mathematical framework involves von Neumann algebras ($M, N$) representing the observable structure, and the physical process is modeled by normal unital positive maps ($T: N \to M$) acting as quantum channels.

**Key observables.** $\alpha$-$z$ quantum Rényi divergences ($D_{\alpha,z}$), the data processing inequality (DPI), and the concept of map sufficiency.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The divergence parameter $\alpha$ and the auxiliary parameter $z$ are crucial, alongside the structure of the von Neumann algebras.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial theorems assume faithfulness of the involved states, though the work aims to relax this. The analysis heavily utilizes the structure of $JW^*$-subalgebras.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops from general results on the DPI to specific recovery theorems for Rényi divergences, utilizing the algebraic structure of $JW^*$-subalgebras and $L^p$-spaces to generalize previous findings.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We prove recovery theorems for $α$-$z$ Rényi divergences under normal unital positive maps between von Neumann algebras. Previous results in this setting required 2-positivity, while recent finite dimensional work showed that this assumption can be relaxed to mere positivity. Our proof uses sufficiency for JW*-subalgebras and $L^p$-spaces over them to characterize equality in the data processing inequality. As a further application of our methods, we solve a problem discussed by Haagerup and Stormer: Every conditional expectation of a von Neumann algebra onto a JW*-subalgebra factors through a conditional expectation onto the generated von Neumann subalgebra.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper benchmarks various quantum feature encoding strategies for use with Quantum Support Vector Machines on five different datasets. It finds that while incorporating data statistics into the encoding can improve performance, overly complex circuits do not guarantee better results. Ultimately, the best strategy requires balancing predictive accuracy with the actual resource cost of the quantum circuit.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on ML, the work directly addresses the resource cost (circuit depth/gates) versus performance trade-off in quantum algorithms, which is crucial for designing practical quantum circuits.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how different methods of encoding classical data into quantum states affect the performance of Quantum Support Vector Machines (QSVM) for binary classification.

**Main result.** The choice of quantum feature encoding strategy is highly dependent on the underlying data structure, and a composite metric balancing predictive performance with quantum circuit complexity is necessary for proper evaluation.

**Method.** The authors benchmarked various feature maps, comparing standard encodings against novel methods that incorporate statistical dependencies (like Pearson or Mutual Information) using controlled gates.

**Model / system.** The model is a quantum computing framework utilizing QSVM. The experiments are simulated using state vector based numerical computation on five distinct binary classification datasets.

**Key observables.** Accuracy, F1 score, ROC-AUC, log-loss, circuit depth, and gate counts.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The statistical dependency measures used include Pearson correlation, Spearman correlation, Mutual Information, and Distance Correlation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The results are based on the state vector representation of ideal quantum states and do not reflect noise or sampling uncertainty from physical hardware.

**Figures summary.** The notes reference tables (Table 24, 25, 26) showing the Quantum Feature Encoding Score (QFES) for specific datasets, comparing various encoding strategies.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares multiple quantum feature encoding strategies (e.g., Angle-RY, Z feature Map, RY+CRY-Pearson) across five datasets, culminating in the use of a composite QFES metric to guide the selection of the optimal encoding.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The way in which classical data are encoded into quantum states plays a significant role in both classification performance and quantum circuit complexity in Quantum Machine Learning. In this study, the effects of different quantum feature encoding strategies on Quantum Support Vector Machine performance were investigated using five binary classification datasets. In particular, the statistical relationships between features were incorporated into quantum circuits through \(RY(θ)\) and controlled-\(RY(θ)\) gates, and this approach was compared with conventional quantum feature maps. The results demonstrate that incorporating statistical relationships into the encoding process can influence classification performance. However, more complex and densely entangled circuits do not necessarily yield higher performance. In addition, a composite evaluation metric was employed to jointly assess predictive performance, generalization, and circuit cost. The findings across the five datasets indicate that the choice of quantum feature encoding strategy should account for the underlying structure of the data and that predictive performance should be evaluated together with quantum circuit complexity.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper uses Quantum Monte Carlo simulations to determine if a He-3 atom forms a bound state at the surface of superfluid He-4. It finds a robust bound state in 3D but demonstrates that this state disappears entirely when the system is reduced to two dimensions. The study also extends these findings to small clusters, showing strong dependence on the substrate material.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides detailed insights into how dimensionality and confinement affect quantum surface phenomena, which is crucial for understanding quantum adsorption and surface-mediated interactions in quantum fluids.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the existence and properties of a bound state for a single He-3 atom interacting with the surface of superfluid He-4, comparing 3D, 2D, and nanoscale cluster geometries.

**Main result.** A bound state exists at the 3D free surface with a binding energy of ~4 K, but this state vanishes in 2D and is highly dependent on the substrate interaction in nanoscale clusters.

**Method.** The research primarily employs Quantum Monte Carlo (QMC) simulations, specifically using the continuous-space Worm Algorithm, to calculate thermodynamic properties and binding energies.

**Model / system.** The system models He-3 atoms interacting with He-4 superfluid surfaces, ranging from infinite 3D free surfaces to finite nanoscale clusters adsorbed on alkali substrates (Cs, Li).

**Key observables.** Binding energy ($\Delta$ or $\epsilon$), He-4 density profiles ($ho(z)$), and the He-3 probability density of position ($|\Psi_3(z)|^2$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T$), dimensionality (3D vs 2D), and the strength of the substrate interaction.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The simulations use established interatomic potentials (e.g., Aziz pair potential) and rely on QMC techniques to handle the quantum nature of the superfluid.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate density profiles and the spatial localization of the He-3 atom at various temperatures and for different cluster sizes/substrates, showing confinement near the surface edge.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares results across different dimensionalities: first establishing the 3D free surface case, then contrasting it with the 2D case, and finally extending the analysis to finite nanoscale clusters on various substrates.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum Monte Carlo simulations confirm the existence of a bound state of a single He-3 atom at a free superfluid He-4 surface in three dimensions, localized within a ~10-A thick low-density He-4 surface layer, with a binding energy of approximately 4 K with respect to vacuum. As the temperature is raised above ~ 1 K the He-3 atom leaves the surface to dissolve into the superfluid. The situation is entirely different in two dimensions, as the He-3 atom is excluded from the superfluid and no surface bound state exists. Results are also presented for the a He-3 atom binding to nanoscale size He-4 clusters adsorbed on weak substrates, a physical system that may allow for the observation of some of the physics predicted for extended surfaces. It is shown that a He-3 bound state localized at the perimeter of sufficiently small (a few tens of atoms) clusters exists not just on Cs, as previously reported, but on all alkali substrates.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper explores how engineering interfaces in oxide superlattices, specifically $	ext{NdNiO}_3/	ext{NdMnO}_3$, can induce novel electronic and magnetic states. By combining transport measurements with X-ray spectroscopy, the authors demonstrate that interfacial charge transfer drives orbital reconstruction, leading to a stable, emergent ferromagnetic insulating phase. This establishes a pathway for designing functional spin-insulatronic materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides experimental evidence of how interfacial chemistry dictates emergent magnetic and electronic phases in correlated oxides, which is highly relevant to understanding strongly correlated quantum materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how charge transfer and competing symmetry breaking drive orbital reconstruction and emergent ferromagnetism in insulating oxide superlattices.

**Main result.** Electron transfer from $	ext{NdMnO}_3$ to $	ext{NdNiO}_3$ drives a room-temperature insulating state with orbital reversal, stabilizing an emergent ferromagnetic insulating state via interfacial superexchange.

**Method.** The research combines structural characterization (XRD, AFM) with advanced electronic measurements, including temperature-dependent transport and element-specific X-ray spectroscopy (XAS/HAXPES).

**Model / system.** The physical system is superlattices formed by alternating layers of $	ext{NdNiO}_3$ and Mott-insulating $	ext{NdMnO}_3$, allowing investigation of interface effects in strongly correlated $3d$ transition-metal oxides.

**Key observables.** Metal-Insulator Transition (MIT) temperature, orbital octahedral rotation patterns, $	ext{Ni}^{2+}/	ext{Ni}^{3+}$ and $	ext{Mn}^{3+}/	ext{Mn}^{4+}$ ratios from XAS, and electronic band structure shifts from HAXPES.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Superlattice periodicity ($n=2, 4, 6$), temperature ($T$), and the nature of the interface charge transfer.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that interfacial charge transfer and orbital overlap dictate the ground state, overriding simple epitaxial strain effects.

**Figures summary.** Figures show XRD scans confirming superlattice periodicity and octahedral rotation modulation, alongside temperature-dependent electrical transport measurements illustrating the suppression of the MIT with increasing periodicity.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically varies the superlattice periodicity ($n$) and measures transport and spectroscopy to correlate interfacial charge transfer (XAS) with electronic state changes (HAXPES) and magnetic ordering (emergent ferromagnetism).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Electron correlation, hopping, and ligand-to-metal charge transfer collectively lead to diverse electronic and magnetic phenomena in 3$d$ transition-metal oxides, where directional d orbitals make hopping highly sensitive to symmetry-dependent orbital overlap. Heterostructure engineering with atomically flat interfaces adds symmetry-breaking charge transfer as a further route to emergent behavior, yet whether interfacial mismatch between constituent oxides of a superlattice shapes ground states independent of epitaxial strain remains unresolved. Here we examine superlattices combining NdNiO$_3$ with Mott-insulating NdMnO$_3$. Varying layer thickness and combining transport with X-ray spectroscopy, we show that electron transfer from NdMnO$_3$ to NdNiO$_3$ drives a room-temperature insulating state with a distinct electronic structure, accompanied by a reversal in orbital symmetry beyond simple strain considerations, underscoring the interface's central role. These reconstructions stabilize an emergent ferromagnetic insulating state arising from interfacial Ni$^{2+}$-O-Mn$^{4+}$ superexchange. Our results establish a pathway to interface-engineered ferromagnetic insulating phases via competing interactions, with potential for spin-insulatronic applications.

</details>

**Authors:** Remi A. Chou  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.28500v1>
